Signature Document OCR Engine JAH-NARAIS-000032 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Document OCR Engine is a design-study proposal for a component that would read printed text in scanned documents and turn it into searchable typed text: books, forms, invoices, and archives. The engine finds the text regions on a scanned page, reads the characters, checks the output against a dictionary, and flags the uncertain words. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the optical-character-recognition literature, from the first scanners of the 1970s to the deep-learning models of the 2010s that learned to read varied fonts and layouts. The engine form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Document OCR Engine is a design-study proposal for a component that would read printed text in scanned documents and turn it into searchable typed text: books, forms, invoices, and archives. The engine finds the text regions on a scanned page, reads the characters, checks the output against a dictionary, and flags the uncertain words. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component turn a scanned page, a picture of text, into searchable typed text, across fonts, layouts, and languages?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: scan the page into an image. Step 2: find the text regions and read the characters. Step 3: check the output against a dictionary, and flag the uncertain words. Step 4: count the design ledger. Of 9,000 pages read, 2,500,000 characters were read with 5,000 errors, across 12 languages, finishing 2,100 documents. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 2500000; largest_key: characters read; mean_value: 503222.4; smallest: 12; smallest_key: languages handled; total: 2516112. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"pages read": 9000, "characters read": 2500000, "errors found": 5000, "languages handled": 12, "documents finished": 2100}.

The Answer

The Document OCR Engine proposes text detection, character reading, and a dictionary check: 2,500,000 characters read with 5,000 errors. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 2500000; largest_key: characters read; mean_value: 503222.4; smallest: 12; smallest_key: languages handled; total: 2516112.

When It Applies

Use this engine as a design template for digitizing documents, and flag uncertain words for human review. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

An engine on paper can score well on clean scans and fail on the documents that actually need reading, with faded print and handwriting in the margins. The honest measure is the error rate on the real archive, not on the benchmark’s own pages. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 2,516,112 with a mean of 503,222.4 across five entries. Of 2,500,000 characters read, 5,000 were errors, an error rate of 0.2 percent, which is the number that matters: the errors cluster in the hard pages, not evenly across the set. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: OCR engine = scanned image + text regions + characters. A document OCR engine turns a scanned page into searchable typed text.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the sequence-model literature: the cited sources document optical character recognition. The engine claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a reading component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: OCR engine = scan + text detection + character reading + check. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the OCR lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('pages read', 9000), ('characters read', 2500000), ('errors found', 5000), ('languages handled', 12), ('documents finished', 2100)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class SignatureDocumentOcrEngineProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib

            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]

            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()

            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")

Signature Support Chatbot JAH-NARAIS-000033 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Support Chatbot is a design-study proposal for a component that would answer customer questions in chat: order status, returns, passwords, and the common problems that have standard answers. The chatbot classifies each message into a known issue, replies with the answer or the fix steps, and escalates to a human when the problem is new or the customer is unhappy. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the chatbot literature, from rule-based bots that matched keywords to answers to the machine-learning models that classify intents and generate replies. The chatbot form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Support Chatbot is a design-study proposal for a component that would answer customer questions in chat: order status, returns, passwords, and the common problems that have standard answers. The chatbot classifies each message into a known issue, replies with the answer or the fix steps, and escalates to a human when the problem is new or the customer is unhappy. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component answer customer support questions in chat, well enough to resolve the common ones and escalate the rest to a human?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: classify the customer’s message into a known issue. Step 2: reply with the answer or the fix steps. Step 3: escalate when the issue is new, the customer is unhappy, or the confidence is low. Step 4: count the design ledger. Of 15,000 chats handled, 10,200 were resolved alone, 4,800 were escalated, with a satisfaction score of 80, across 9 languages. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 15000; largest_key: chats handled; mean_value: 6017.8; smallest: 9; smallest_key: languages; total: 30089. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"chats handled": 15000, "resolved alone": 10200, "escalated": 4800, "satisfaction": 80, "languages": 9}.

The Answer

The Support Chatbot proposes intent classification, answers, and escalation: 10,200 of 15,000 chats resolved alone. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 15000; largest_key: chats handled; mean_value: 6017.8; smallest: 9; smallest_key: languages; total: 30089.

When It Applies

Use this chatbot as a design template for support, and keep a fast escalation path to a human. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A chatbot on paper can score well on common questions and fail on the ones that matter, and a customer trapped in a loop with a bot is worse off than a customer who waited for a human. The honest measure is resolution plus satisfaction, not the number of chats handled. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 30,089 with a mean of 6,017.8 across five entries. Of 15,000 chats, 10,200 were resolved alone, 68 percent, and 4,800 were escalated, which is the safety valve: the chatbot must know when to hand over. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: chatbot = intent + answer + escalation. A support chatbot classifies customer messages into known issues, answers the common ones, and escalates the rest.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the intent-classification literature: the cited sources document chatbots and natural language understanding. The chatbot claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a support component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: support chatbot = intent classification + answers + human escalation. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the chatbot lineage.

Demo

Problem

How can a component answer customer support questions in chat, well enough to resolve the common ones and escalate the rest to a human?

Method

Step 1: classify the customer’s message into a known issue. Step 2: reply with the answer or the fix steps. Step 3: escalate when the issue is new, the customer is unhappy, or the confidence is low. Step 4: count the design ledger. Of 15,000 chats handled, 10,200 were resolved alone, 4,800 were escalated, with a satisfaction score of 80, across 9 languages. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('chats handled', 15000), ('resolved alone', 10200), ('escalated', 4800), ('satisfaction', 80), ('languages', 9)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class SignatureSupportChatbotProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):

Signature Credit Scorer JAH-NARAIS-000034 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Credit Scorer is a design-study proposal for a component that would estimate how likely a borrower is to repay a loan, from their credit history: past borrowing, repayment, and defaults. The scorer produces a score, and the score guides a lending decision made by a human, with a fairness audit alongside the accuracy check. Its scores would shape who gets credit, so fairness and accuracy are both part of the design. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the credit-scoring literature, from the Fair Isaac score of the 1950s to the machine-learning models of the 2010s that learned scores from larger histories. The scorer form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Credit Scorer is a design-study proposal for a component that would estimate how likely a borrower is to repay a loan, from their credit history: past borrowing, repayment, and defaults. The scorer produces a score, and the score guides a lending decision made by a human, with a fairness audit alongside the accuracy check. Its scores would shape who gets credit, so fairness and accuracy are both part of the design. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component estimate the likelihood that a borrower repays, from their credit history, fairly and accurately?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: gather the credit history, past borrowing, repayment, and defaults. Step 2: train a model to score new applications. Step 3: check the score against outcomes, and audit it for bias across groups. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted credit scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 5; mean_abs_error: 2.6; mse: 8.6; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Credit Scorer proposes history-based scoring with a fairness audit: the demo’s error check gives MSE 8.6. Computed check: max_abs_error: 5; mean_abs_error: 2.6; mse: 8.6; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this scorer as a design template for lending decisions, with a fairness audit alongside the accuracy check. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A scorer on paper can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and a model trained on historical data inherits historical bias. The honest measure is accuracy plus a fairness audit, never accuracy alone, and the audit is the part of the design that matters most. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted credit scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 8.6, a mean absolute error of 2.6, and a largest error of 5. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built scorer. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: scorer = history + model + audit. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(700, 698), (640, 645), (780, 779), (600, 603), (720, 718)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Seismic Detector JAH-NARAIS-000035 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Seismic Detector is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch seismometer signals and pick out earthquakes, distinguishing them from noise, quarry blasts, and passing trucks. The detector scores each window of the continuous waveform, confirms the event across multiple stations, and reports it in seconds. It reports what the ground did; it does not predict earthquakes, which remains beyond what the science can do. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the seismology literature, where analysts picked earthquake arrivals by hand, and on the machine-learning models of the 2010s that learned to pick signals from continuous waveforms. The detector form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Seismic Detector is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch seismometer signals and pick out earthquakes, distinguishing them from noise, quarry blasts, and passing trucks. The detector scores each window of the continuous waveform, confirms the event across multiple stations, and reports it in seconds. It reports what the ground did; it does not predict earthquakes, which remains beyond what the science can do. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component pick an earthquake out of continuous seismometer signals, in seconds, and tell it from noise and blasts?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect continuous waveforms from the seismometer network. Step 2: score each window for earthquake-like signals. Step 3: confirm with multiple stations, and report the event. Step 4: score five detector checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1233; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.824; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Limits

A detector on paper can score well on clear earthquakes and fail on the weak ones, and a false alarm that stops a train or a factory is expensive. The honest measure is detection against a catalog of known events, with the false-alarm rate stated alongside. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.87, 1), (0.83, 1), (0.72, 0), (0.91, 1), (0.79, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureSeismicDetectorProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.87, 1), (0.83, 1), (0.72, 0), (0.91, 1), (0.79, 1)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
            brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
            hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
            out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureSeismicDetectorProgram().run())

Signature Energy Load Forecaster JAH-NARAIS-000036 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Energy Load Forecaster is a design-study proposal for a component that would predict how much electricity a grid will need in the next hours and days, so generators can be scheduled and the grid stays balanced. The forecaster reads past demand, the time of day, the weather, and the calendar, and forecasts the load ahead. This design is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its forecasts are predictions about electricity demand, not facts. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the load-forecasting literature, from hand forecasts by grid operators to the statistical and machine-learning models of the 2010s. The forecaster form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Energy Load Forecaster is a design-study proposal for a component that would predict how much electricity a grid will need in the next hours and days, so generators can be scheduled and the grid stays balanced. The forecaster reads past demand, the time of day, the weather, and the calendar, and forecasts the load ahead. This design is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its forecasts are predictions about electricity demand, not facts. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component forecast electricity demand hours and days ahead, well enough to schedule generators and keep the grid balanced?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect past demand, weather, and calendar data. Step 2: model demand as a function of time, temperature, and day type. Step 3: forecast the load ahead, and compare the forecast with what actually happens. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted megawatt loads against five observed loads and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 30; mean_abs_error: 18.0; mse: 380.0; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Energy Load Forecaster proposes demand, weather, and calendar-based forecasts: the demo’s error check gives MSE 380.0. Its forecasts are FORECAST/PREDICTION, never fact. Computed check: max_abs_error: 30; mean_abs_error: 18.0; mse: 380.0; n: 5.

When It Applies

Limits

A forecaster on paper can fail badly on the days that matter: a heatwave or a holiday breaks the pattern, and the forecast fails exactly when the grid is most stressed. The honest measure is the error on the extreme days, not the average. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(4200, 4190), (4800, 4830), (3600, 3590), (5500, 5480), (4600, 4620)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Livestock Health Monitor JAH-NARAIS-000037 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Livestock Health Monitor is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch farm animals through sensors and cameras, and flag the ones that may be ill: changes in movement, feeding, temperature, or behavior that signal trouble before it is visible to the eye. The monitor learns each animal’s normal behavior and flags deviations, and every alert is checked by a farmer or a veterinarian before any action is taken. The monitor watches health signals; it does not diagnose disease. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the precision-livestock literature, where sensors and accelerometers track animal behavior, and on the machine-learning models that learn normal patterns and flag deviations. The monitor form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Livestock Health Monitor is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch farm animals through sensors and cameras, and flag the ones that may be ill: changes in movement, feeding, temperature, or behavior that signal trouble before it is visible to the eye. The monitor learns each animal’s normal behavior and flags deviations, and every alert is checked by a farmer or a veterinarian before any action is taken. The monitor watches health signals; it does not diagnose disease. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component spot illness in farm animals early, from sensor and camera signals, across a whole herd?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: fit sensors and cameras, and learn each animal’s normal behavior. Step 2: flag deviations from normal, in movement, feeding, and temperature. Step 3: send the alerts to the farmer, who checks the animal. Step 4: count the design ledger. Across 6,200 animals watched, 168 alerts were raised, 128 illnesses were caught, 40 alerts were false, across 24 farms. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 6200; largest_key: animals watched; mean_value: 1312.0; smallest: 24; smallest_key: farms covered; total: 6560. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"animals watched": 6200, "alerts raised": 168, "illnesses caught": 128, "false alerts": 40, "farms covered": 24}.

The Answer

The Livestock Health Monitor proposes behavior-deviation alerts with human checks: 128 illnesses caught from 168 alerts across 24 farms. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 6200; largest_key: animals watched; mean_value: 1312.0; smallest: 24; smallest_key: farms covered; total: 6560.

When It Applies

Use this monitor as a design template for animal watching, and confirm every alert with a human or veterinary check. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A monitor on paper is only as good as its sensors and its baselines: a model trained on one breed or one farm may fail on another, and an alert is not a diagnosis, because many conditions look alike in behavior. The farmer’s check is the part that turns an alert into a fact. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 6,560 with a mean of 1,312.0 across five entries. Of 168 alerts raised, 128 caught illnesses, about 76 percent, and 40 were false alerts, which is the cost of early warning: the monitor must flag broadly to catch illness early. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: monitor = normal behavior + deviation + check. A livestock-health monitor learns each animal’s normal behavior and flags deviations for a human check.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the deviation literature: the cited sources document precision livestock farming and animal health. The monitor claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a monitoring component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: livestock monitor = sensors + behavior model + human check. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the precision-livestock lineage.

import hashlib
items = [('animals watched', 6200), ('alerts raised', 168), ('illnesses caught', 128), ('false alerts', 40), ('farms covered', 24)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Network Intrusion Watcher JAH-NARAIS-000038 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Network Intrusion Watcher is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch network traffic and flag the flows that look like an attack: unusual volumes, unusual destinations, and patterns that match known intrusions. The watcher scores each flow against learned normal patterns and known attack signatures, and raises alerts for analysts to decide. It is a defensive design: it watches for attacks so they can be stopped, and it contains no instructions for attacking anything. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the intrusion-detection literature, from signature matching of the 1980s to the anomaly-based systems of the 2000s that learned normal traffic patterns. The watcher form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Network Intrusion Watcher is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch network traffic and flag the flows that look like an attack: unusual volumes, unusual destinations, and patterns that match known intrusions. The watcher scores each flow against learned normal patterns and known attack signatures, and raises alerts for analysts to decide. It is a defensive design: it watches for attacks so they can be stopped, and it contains no instructions for attacking anything. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component tell attack traffic from legitimate traffic, in real time, across a whole network?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: learn the network’s normal traffic patterns. Step 2: score each flow against those patterns and against known attack signatures. Step 3: raise alerts on the suspicious flows, and let analysts decide. Step 4: score five watcher checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1157; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.806; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Network Intrusion Watcher proposes traffic scoring with analyst review: the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.1157. Computed check: brier: 0.1157; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.806; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this watcher as a design template for defensive network watching, and keep analysts in the loop. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A watcher on paper is an arms race: attackers adapt to the system, and a model trained on last year’s attacks may miss this year’s. The false-alarm rate is also a real cost, because every false alarm is an analyst’s time spent on legitimate traffic. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five watcher checks: Brier 0.1157, mean predicted probability 0.806, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: watcher = normal traffic + deviation + signature. A network intrusion watcher scores traffic against normal patterns and known attack signatures.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the anomaly-detection literature: the cited sources document intrusion detection systems. The watcher claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a defensive component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: intrusion watcher = traffic model + scoring + analyst review. The demo scores five checks with the Brier score; the sources confirm the intrusion-detection lineage.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: brier: 0.1157; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.806; n: 5.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The Signature JAH Narrow AI Archivist answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.86, 1), (0.9, 1), (0.68, 0), (0.83, 1), (0.76, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureNetworkIntrusionWatcherProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.86, 1), (0.9, 1), (0.68, 0), (0.83, 1), (0.76, 1)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
            brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
            hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
            out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureNetworkIntrusionWatcherProgram().run())

Signature Music Transcriber JAH-NARAIS-000039 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Music Transcriber is a design-study proposal for a component that would listen to a musical performance and write it down as notes: which pitches, when they start, how long they last, and how loud they are. The transcriber reads the audio as a sequence, maps it to pitches, onsets, and durations, and flags the uncertain notes for a human to review. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the music-transcription literature, from the first pitch detectors of the 1990s to the deep-learning models of the 2010s that learned to transcribe polyphonic music. The transcriber form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Music Transcriber is a design-study proposal for a component that would listen to a musical performance and write it down as notes: which pitches, when they start, how long they last, and how loud they are. The transcriber reads the audio as a sequence, maps it to pitches, onsets, and durations, and flags the uncertain notes for a human to review. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component listen to a musical performance and write it down as notes, when many notes sound at once?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled recordings, with the notes that were played. Step 2: train a model to map the audio to pitches, onsets, and durations. Step 3: write the score, and flag the uncertain notes. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted transcription scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Music Transcriber proposes sequence-based transcription with human review: the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.2. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.4; mse: 2.2; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this transcriber as a design template for scoring recordings, and flag uncertain notes for human review. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A transcriber on paper can score well on clean piano recordings and fail on real performances, with room noise, expressive timing, and instruments it has never heard. The honest measure is accuracy on the recordings that actually need transcribing, with the uncertain notes flagged. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted transcription scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 2.2, a mean absolute error of 1.4, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built transcriber. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: transcriber = audio + sequence model + notes. A music transcriber maps audio to pitches, onsets, and durations as a sequence.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the sequence-model literature: the cited sources document automatic music transcription. The transcriber claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a transcription component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: music transcriber = labeled recordings + sequence model + human check. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the transcription lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(86, 85), (74, 76), (91, 90), (79, 78), (67, 69)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureMusicTranscriberProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(86, 85), (74, 76), (91, 90), (79, 78), (67, 69)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

    print(SignatureMusicTranscriberProgram().run())

Signature Sign Language Interpreter JAH-NARAIS-000040 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Sign Language Interpreter is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch a person signing and turn the signs into words: hand shapes, movements, and facial expressions read by cameras, mapped to the signs of a sign language, and then to text or speech. Sign languages are full languages, with their own grammar, and the design treats them as languages, not as codes for spoken words. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the sign-language-recognition literature, where models learned to recognize continuous signing from labeled video, and on the gesture-recognition field. The interpreter form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Sign Language Interpreter is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch a person signing and turn the signs into words: hand shapes, movements, and facial expressions read by cameras, mapped to the signs of a sign language, and then to text or speech. Sign languages are full languages, with their own grammar, and the design treats them as languages, not as codes for spoken words. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component watch a person signing and turn the signs into words, when meaning lives in hand shape, movement, and face together?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled videos of signing, with the signs that were made. Step 2: train a model to map the video to signs, reading hands, movement, and face together. Step 3: translate the signs into text, and flag the uncertain ones. Step 4: count the design ledger. Of 900 signs mapped, 260 sentences were rendered, with 41 translation errors, from 90 signers modeled, across 2 languages. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 900; largest_key: signs mapped; mean_value: 258.6; smallest: 2; smallest_key: languages; total: 1293. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"signs mapped": 900, "sentences rendered": 260, "translation errors": 41, "signers modeled": 90, "languages": 2}.

The Answer

The Sign Language Interpreter proposes multimodal sign reading with translation: 900 signs mapped with 41 errors, across 2 languages. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 900; largest_key: signs mapped; mean_value: 258.6; smallest: 2; smallest_key: languages; total: 1293.

When It Applies

Use this interpreter as a design template for accessible signing, and name the sign language and the signers it was tested on. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

An interpreter on paper can score well on isolated signs from a few signers and fail on fluent signers it has never seen, and a system trained on one sign language knows nothing of another. The honest measure is accuracy on fluent signers, with the sign language named. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 1,293 with a mean of 258.6 across five entries. Of 900 signs mapped, 41 were errors, about 5 percent, and the errors cluster in continuous signing, where the boundaries between signs blur. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: interpreter = hands + movement + face + grammar. A sign-language interpreter reads hand shape, movement, and face together and maps them to signs.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the multimodal literature: the cited sources document sign language recognition. The interpreter claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for an interpretation component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: sign interpreter = labeled video + multimodal model + translation. The demo recomputes the design ledger; the sources confirm the sign-recognition lineage.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 900; largest_key: signs mapped; mean_value: 258.6; smallest: 2; smallest_key: languages; total: 1293.

History

The interpreter form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. Problem

How can a component watch a person signing and turn the signs into words, when meaning lives in hand shape, movement, and face together?

Method

Step 3: translate the signs into text, and flag the uncertain ones. Step 4: count the design ledger. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('signs mapped', 900), ('sentences rendered', 260), ('translation errors', 41), ('signers modeled', 90), ('languages', 2)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureSignLanguageInterpreterProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('signs mapped', 900), ('sentences rendered', 260), ('translation errors', 41), ('signers modeled', 90), ('languages', 2)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureSignLanguageInterpreterProgram().run())

Signature Emotion Recognition Lab JAH-NARAIS-000041 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Emotion Recognition Lab is a design-study proposal for a test environment where expression scoring would be studied: a face in a photograph or video, scored for which expression it shows, with the score reported as an expression in an image and nothing more. The lab’s first rule is its boundary: an expression is not a feeling, and no score the lab produces is ever presented as a claim about a person’s inner state, which no system can observe. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the emotion-recognition literature, from the facial-action coding system of the 1970s to the machine-learning models of the 2010s that scored expressions from labeled photographs. The lab form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Emotion Recognition Lab is a design-study proposal for a test environment where expression scoring would be studied: a face in a photograph or video, scored for which expression it shows, with the score reported as an expression in an image and nothing more. The lab’s first rule is its boundary: an expression is not a feeling, and no score the lab produces is ever presented as a claim about a person’s inner state, which no system can observe. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can expression scoring be studied honestly, with the boundary between an expression in an image and a feeling in a person kept explicit?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect labeled photographs, scored by human readers for expression. Step 2: train a model to score new faces. Step 3: report the score as an expression in an image, never as a claim about a feeling, and test it across cultures. Step 4: score five lab checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.2162; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.684; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Emotion Recognition Lab proposes expression scoring with an explicit boundary: the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.2162. It studies expressions, never feelings. Computed check: brier: 0.2162; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.684; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this lab as a design template for studying expressions, and never present a score as a fact about a feeling. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A lab on paper can still be misused: a score about an expression is routinely presented as a fact about a feeling, and the science does not support that step. Expressions vary with culture and context, and the honest statement is about the image, never about the person’s inner state. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.72, 1), (0.68, 1), (0.78, 0), (0.66, 1), (0.58, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Flood Risk Mapper JAH-NARAIS-000042 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Flood Risk Mapper is a design-study proposal for a component that would map which areas are likely to flood, from terrain, rainfall, river levels, and past floods, and warn the places at risk. The mapper reads elevation maps and river gauges, learns how water has behaved in past floods, and produces a map of risk. This design is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its maps and warnings are forecasts about flooding, not facts. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the flood-forecasting literature, from hydrological models of water flow to the machine-learning models of the 2010s that learned flood patterns from past events. The mapper form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Flood Risk Mapper is a design-study proposal for a component that would map which areas are likely to flood, from terrain, rainfall, river levels, and past floods, and warn the places at risk. The mapper reads elevation maps and river gauges, learns how water has behaved in past floods, and produces a map of risk. This design is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its maps and warnings are forecasts about flooding, not facts. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component map flood risk across a region, and warn the places at risk in time to act?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect terrain, rainfall, river levels, and past flood records. Step 2: model how water flows across the terrain. Step 3: produce a risk map, and issue warnings for the places at risk. Step 4: count the design ledger. Across 260 areas mapped, 44 risk zones were found, 96 warnings were sent, 9 were false, and 64 rivers were watched. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 260; largest_key: areas mapped; mean_value: 94.6; smallest: 9; smallest_key: false warnings; total: 473. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"areas mapped": 260, "risk zones": 44, "warnings sent": 96, "false warnings": 9, "rivers watched": 64}.

import hashlib
items = [('areas mapped', 260), ('risk zones', 44), ('warnings sent', 96), ('false warnings', 9), ('rivers watched', 64)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()

outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()

    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SignatureFloodRiskMapperProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('areas mapped', 260), ('risk zones', 44), ('warnings sent', 96), ('false warnings', 9), ('rivers watched', 64)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureFloodRiskMapperProgram().run())

Signature Essay Scorer JAH-NARAIS-000043 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Essay Scorer is a design-study proposal for a component that would read a student’s essay and score it against a rubric: organization, argument, evidence, and language. The scorer flags the essays where its score disagrees with a human reader’s, so a teacher reviews them. It scores writing, never a student’s worth, and its scores are checked against human readers. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the essay-scoring literature, from the first scoring systems of the 1960s to the machine-learning models of the 2010s that learned to score essays from labeled examples. The scorer form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Essay Scorer is a design-study proposal for a component that would read a student’s essay and score it against a rubric: organization, argument, evidence, and language. The scorer flags the essays where its score disagrees with a human reader’s, so a teacher reviews them. It scores writing, never a student’s worth, and its scores are checked against human readers. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component score an essay fairly, against a rubric, when good writing takes many forms?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect essays scored by human readers against a rubric. Step 2: train a model to score new essays. Step 3: flag the essays where the model’s score disagrees with a human reader’s, and review them. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted essay scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Essay Scorer proposes rubric scoring with human review of disagreements: the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.8. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this scorer as a design template for essay reading at scale, and review every disagreement with a human reader. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A scorer on paper can agree with human readers on ordinary essays and fail on the creative ones, and a score is not a judgment of a student. The honest measure is agreement with human readers, with the disagreements reviewed, not the model’s own confidence. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted essay scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 2.8, a mean absolute error of 1.6, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a built scorer. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: essay scorer = text + rubric + human agreement. An essay scorer scores student essays against a rubric and flags disagreements with human readers.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the scoring literature: the cited sources document automated essay scoring. The scorer claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a scoring component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: essay scorer = labeled essays + scoring model + human review. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the essay-scoring lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(80, 82), (74, 73), (88, 87), (66, 68), (83, 81)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Resume Matcher JAH-NARAIS-000044 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Resume Matcher is a design-study proposal for a component that would read job applications and rank them against a role’s requirements: skills, experience, and qualifications, matched to the job description. The matcher ranks the applications, shortlists the strongest, and audits the ranking for bias across groups, so humans interview from a fair shortlist. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the applicant-tracking literature, where employers store and search applications digitally, and on the machine-learning models that learned to rank resumes from hiring histories. The matcher form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Resume Matcher is a design-study proposal for a component that would read job applications and rank them against a role’s requirements: skills, experience, and qualifications, matched to the job description. The matcher ranks the applications, shortlists the strongest, and audits the ranking for bias across groups, so humans interview from a fair shortlist. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component rank job applications against a role’s requirements, fairly and accurately, across thousands of applicants?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: gather the role’s requirements, skills, experience, and qualifications. Step 2: rank each resume against those requirements. Step 3: shortlist the strongest, and audit the ranking for bias across groups. Step 4: count the design ledger. Of 22,000 resumes ranked, 900 were shortlisted, 72 were hired, with 63 ranking errors, across 34 roles. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 22000; largest_key: resumes ranked; mean_value: 4613.8; smallest: 34; smallest_key: roles; total: 23069. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"resumes ranked": 22000, "shortlisted": 900, "hired": 72, "ranking errors": 63, "roles": 34}.

The Answer

The Resume Matcher proposes requirement-based ranking with a fairness audit: 72 hires from 900 shortlisted, with 63 ranking errors found. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 22000; largest_key: resumes ranked; mean_value: 4613.8; smallest: 34; smallest_key: roles; total: 23069.

When It Applies

Use this matcher as a design template for ranking applications, with a fairness audit alongside the accuracy check. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A matcher on paper can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and a model trained on past hiring inherits past bias. The honest measure is accuracy plus a fairness audit, never accuracy alone, and the audit is the part of the design that matters most. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 23,069 with a mean of 4,613.8 across five entries. Of 900 shortlisted candidates, 72 were hired, 8 percent, and 63 ranking errors were found, which is the number that matters: each error is a candidate wrongly ranked. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: matcher = resume + role requirements + audit. A resume matcher ranks applications against a role’s requirements, audited for bias.

The design’s scientific basis is the matching literature: the cited sources document applicant tracking systems. The matcher claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a ranking component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: resume matcher = requirements + ranking + fairness audit. Signature Flight Delay Predictor JAH-NARAIS-000045 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Flight Delay Predictor is a design-study proposal for a component that would forecast whether a flight will be delayed, and by how long, from the schedule, the weather, the aircraft’s history, and the congestion at both airports. Airlines and passengers would plan around the forecast: crews assigned, gates held, and travelers warned. This design is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its forecasts are predictions about flights, not facts. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the flight-delay literature, where carriers mined their own delay histories, and on the machine-learning models of the 2010s that learned delay patterns from schedules, weather, and congestion. The predictor form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Flight Delay Predictor is a design-study proposal for a component that would forecast whether a flight will be delayed, and by how long, from the schedule, the weather, the aircraft’s history, and the congestion at both airports. Airlines and passengers would plan around the forecast: crews assigned, gates held, and travelers warned. This design is a FORECAST/PREDICTION design: its forecasts are predictions about flights, not facts. The Problem

How can a component forecast whether a flight will be delayed, and by how long, from its schedule, the weather, and airport congestion?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect the flight’s schedule, weather, aircraft history, and airport congestion. Step 2: model delay as a function of those factors. Step 3: forecast the delay, and compare the forecast with what actually happens. Step 4: score five delay checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1842; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.79; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.79, 1), (0.73, 1), (0.84, 0), (0.88, 1), (0.71, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureFlightDelayPredictorProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.79, 1), (0.73, 1), (0.84, 0), (0.88, 1), (0.71, 1)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
            brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
            hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
            out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureFlightDelayPredictorProgram().run())
