Signature Climate Controller JAH-NARAIS-000046 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Climate Controller is a design-study proposal for a component that would learn when a home is occupied and what temperature its people prefer, and heat or cool the home to match, saving energy when nobody is home. The controller reads occupancy, time, and weather, learns the household’s schedule, and adjusts heating and cooling to fit, learning from manual overrides. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the smart-thermostat literature, where thermostats learned household schedules and adjusted heating and cooling automatically. The controller form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Climate Controller is a design-study proposal for a component that would learn when a home is occupied and what temperature its people prefer, and heat or cool the home to match, saving energy when nobody is home. The controller reads occupancy, time, and weather, learns the household’s schedule, and adjusts heating and cooling to fit, learning from manual overrides. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component learn a household’s schedule and preferences, and heat or cool the home to match, saving energy without sacrificing comfort?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: read occupancy, time, and weather. Step 2: learn the household’s schedule and preferred temperatures. Step 3: adjust heating and cooling to fit, and learn from manual overrides. Step 4: count the design ledger. Across 9,000 homes managed, 17 percent energy was saved, with 180 comfort complaints, 8,600 schedules learned, and 640 manual overrides. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 9000; largest_key: homes managed; mean_value: 3687.4; smallest: 17; smallest_key: energy saved; total: 18437. import hashlib
items = [('homes managed', 9000), ('energy saved', 17), ('complaints', 180), ('schedules learned', 8600), ('overrides', 640)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")

print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Document Summarizer is a design-study proposal for a component that would read a long document and write a short version of it: the main points, in fewer words, so a reader can grasp the document without reading it all. The summarizer scores sentences by importance, selects the main points or writes new sentences that state them, and checks the summary against the document, flagging anything the document does not support. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

History

The design builds on the summarization literature, from extractive systems that picked the most important sentences to the abstractive language models of the 2020s that write fluent summaries. The summarizer form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can a component read a long document and write a short version that keeps the main points and invents nothing?

Method

Step 1: read the document and score its sentences by importance. Step 2: select the main points, or write new sentences that state them. Step 3: check the summary against the document, and flag anything the document does not support. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted summary scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted.

Dataset

A Signature-made summarizer design; the demo is a worked error example, not a built summarizer.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

Skeptical analysis

A summarizer on paper can look right while omitting the document’s key caveat, or inventing a point the document never made. The honest measure is the summary checked against the document, with the omissions and inventions counted, not the fluency of the prose. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(84, 83), (77, 78), (89, 88), (72, 74), (81, 80)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SignatureDocumentSummarizerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(84, 83), (77, 78), (89, 88), (72, 74), (81, 80)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SignatureDocumentSummarizerProgram().run())

Signature Gesture Recognizer JAH-NARAIS-000048 · simple

Evidence: design-study A Signature-made design proposal; not a real system, not established fact.

Signature design-study. The Signature Gesture Recognizer is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch a person’s hands and body and turn their movements into commands: a wave to dismiss, a point to select, a swipe to scroll. The recognizer tracks the hands and body in video, classifies the movement into a gesture from a fixed set, and maps it to a command, confirming the risky ones. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

History

The design builds on the gesture-recognition literature, where cameras and depth sensors learned to track hands and bodies in video. The recognizer form is the Signature system’s own arrangement of that lineage, drawn as a design proposal in this record. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Signature design-study. The Signature Gesture Recognizer is a design-study proposal for a component that would watch a person’s hands and body and turn their movements into commands: a wave to dismiss, a point to select, a swipe to scroll. The recognizer tracks the hands and body in video, classifies the movement into a gesture from a fixed set, and maps it to a command, confirming the risky ones. The design exists on paper only, and every claim in it is a proposal, never a statement about a built system.

The Problem

How can a component watch a person’s gestures and turn them into commands, when the same movement can mean different things?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: track the hands and body in video. Step 2: classify the movement into a gesture from a fixed set. Step 3: map the gesture to a command, and confirm the risky ones. Step 4: score five recognizer checks with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.1287; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.816; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

The Gesture Recognizer proposes tracking, classification, and command mapping with confirmation: the ledger’s checks score Brier 0.1287. Computed check: brier: 0.1287; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.816; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use this recognizer as a design template for touch-free control, and confirm the risky commands. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A recognizer on paper can score well on a benchmark of clear gestures and fail on real people, with different bodies, speeds, and habits. The honest measure is accuracy on real users, with the accidental activations counted alongside the correct ones. This is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM, not established fact: the design is a proposal on paper, never a built system, and no claim here is presented as established fact. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five recognizer checks: Brier 0.1287, mean predicted probability 0.816, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: recognizer = movement + fixed set + command. A gesture recognizer maps hand and body movements to commands from a fixed set.

Science Proof

The design’s scientific basis is the classification literature: the cited sources document gesture recognition. The recognizer claims nothing beyond that literature: it is a proposal for a recognition component, labeled as a design-study throughout. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: gesture recognizer = tracking + classification + command mapping. The demo scores five checks with the Brier score; the sources confirm the gesture-recognition lineage.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.86, 1), (0.79, 1), (0.73, 0), (0.88, 1), (0.82, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class SignatureGestureRecognizerProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):

            out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
            return out

        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
